Concrete Branching Bisimilarity for Processes with Time-outs
Gaspard Reghem, Rob J. van Glabbeek

Abstract
This paper provides an adaptation of branching bisimilarity to reactive systems with time-outs that does not enable eliding of time-out transitions. Multiple equivalent definitions are procured, along with a modal characterisation and a proof of its congruence property for a standard process algebra with recursion. The last section presents a complete axiomatisation for guarded processes without infinite sequences of unobservable actions.

1
Introduction

Strong bisimilarity

[
17
]
is the default semantic equivalence on labelled transition systems (LTSs), modelling systems that move from state to state by performing discrete, uninterpreted actions. In
[
11
]
, it has been generalised, under the name
strong reactive bisimilarity
,
to LTSs that feature, besides the hidden action
\tau

[
17
]
, an unobservable
time-out
action
{\rm t}

[
9
]
, modelling the end of a time-consuming activity from which we abstract. This addition significantly increases the expressiveness of the model
[
10
,
11
]
.

Applied to the verification of realistic distributed systems, strong bisimilarity is too fine an equivalence, especially because it does not cater to abstraction from internal activity.
Branching bisimilarity

[
13
]
is a variant that does abstract from internal activity, and lies at the basis of many verification toolsets
[
3
,
6
]
. The present paper, as well as
[
20
]
, generalises branching bisimilarity to LTSs with time-outs, thereby combining the virtues of
[
11
]
and
[
13
]
. The resulting notion of
branching reactive bisimilarity
, proposed in
[
20
]
, elides time-outs, in the sense that—using the process algebra notation to be formally introduced in Section
4
—the processes
a.{\rm t}.b.0
and
a.{\rm t}.{\rm t}.b.0
(as well as
a.{\rm t}.\tau.{\rm t}.b.0
) are branching reactive bisimilar. Both require an unquantified positive but finite amount of rest between the actions
a
and
b
. In this paper we propose a
concrete branching reactive bisimilarity
that instead treats time-outs more like visible actions. We support this notion through a modal characterisation, congruence results for a standard process algebra with recursion, and a complete axiomatisation.

The addition of the time-out action
{\rm t}
aims at modelling the passage of time while staying in the realm of
untimed
process algebra. Here, “untimed” means that our framework does not facilitate measuring time, even though it models whether a system can pause in some state or not. We assume that the execution of any action is instantaneous; thus, time elapses in states only. The amount of time spent in a state is dictated by the interaction of the system with an external entity called its
environment
.

We call a system
reactive
if it interacts with an environment able to allow or disallow visible actions. The environment represents a user or other systems, running in parallel, which has no control over
\tau
or
{\rm t}
actions. If
X
is the set of visible actions currently allowed by the environment and the system can perform any transition labelled by an element of
X\cup\{\tau\}
then it will perform one of those transitions immediately. When a visible action is performed, it triggers the environment to choose a new set of allowed actions. If the environment is allowing
X
and the system cannot perform any transition labelled by
\tau
or any allowed action, then the system is said to be
idling
. When the system idles, time-outs become executable, but the environment can also get impatient and choose a new
X
before any time-out occurs.

We have supposed that the environment cannot synchronise with the execution of a time-out, thus implying that, right after executing a time-out, the environment is still allowing the same set of allowed actions as before this execution. For example, the process
a.P+{\rm t}.(a.Q+\tau.R)
will never reach
Q
because, for the time-out to happen, the environment has to block
a
and so
a.Q+\tau.R
can only be reached when the environment blocks
a
. In this case, the
\tau
-transition is always executed before the environment can allow
a
again.

Similarly, strong and [concrete] branching reactive bisimilarity satisfy the process algebraic law
\tau.P+{\rm t}.Q=\tau.P
, essentially giving
\tau
priority over
{\rm t}
. Whereas this could have been formalised through an operational semantics in which the process
\tau.P+{\rm t}.Q
lacks an outgoing
{\rm t}
-transition, here, and in
[
11
]
, we derive an LTS for a standard process algebra with time-outs in a way that treats
{\rm t}
just like any other action. Instead, the priority of
\tau
over
{\rm t}
is implemented in the reactive bisimilarity: its says that even though the transition

\tau.P+{\rm t}.Q\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}Q
is present in our LTS, it will never be taken. This approach is not only simpler, it also generalises better to choices like
b.P+{\rm t}.Q
, where the priority of
b
over
{\rm t}
is conditional on the environment in which the system is placed, namely on whether or not this environment allows the
b
-action to occur.

From the system’s perspective, the environment can be in two kinds of states: either allowing a specific set of actions, or being triggered to change. Our model does not stipulate how much time the environment takes to choose a new set of allowed actions once triggered, or even if it will ever make such a choice. Thus, the system could perform some transitions while the environment is triggered, especially those labelled
\tau
. In our view, the most natural way to see the environment is as another system executed in parallel, while enforcing synchronisation on all visible actions. This implies that the environment allows a set
X
of actions when it idles in a state whose set of initial actions is
X
, and the environment is triggered when it is not idling, especially when it can perform a
\tau
-transition. In this paradigm, while the environment is triggered, any action can be allowed for a brief amount of time. However, there is no reason to believe that it will necessarily settle down on a specific set. For instance, this can happen if the environment reaches a
divergence
: an infinite sequence of
\tau
-transitions.

In
[
7
]
, seven (or nine) forms of branching bisimilarity are classified; they differ only in the treatment of divergence. In the present paper we are chiefly interested in divergence-free processes, on grounds that in the intuition of
[
11
]
any sequence of
\tau
-transitions could be executed in time zero; yet we do wish to allow infinite sequences of
{\rm t}
-transitions.
For divergence-free process all these forms of branching bisimilarity coincide.
Nevertheless, we do not formally exclude divergences, and in their presence our concrete branching reactive bisimilarity generalises the
stability respecting branching bisimilarity
of
[
7
]
, which differs from the default version from
[
13
]
through the presence of Clause 2.e of Definition
1
. There does not exist a plausible reactive generalisation of the default version.

Section
2
supplies the formal definition of concrete branching reactive bisimilarity as well as its rooted version, which will be shown to be its congruence closure. It also provides equivalent definitions that reduce our bisimilarity to a non-reactive one and illustrate that concrete branching reactive bisimilarity coincides with stability respecting branching bisimilarity in the absence of time-outs.

Section
3
gives a modal characterisation of concrete branching reactive bisimilarity and its rooted version on an extension of the Hennessy-Milner logic. Section
4
introduces the process algebra
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
along with an alternative characterisation of concrete branching reactive bisimilarity that will be used to prove that rooted concrete branching reactive bisimilarity is a full congruence for
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
.

Section
5
displays a complete axiomatisation of our bisimilarity on different fragments of
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
. Most completeness proofs rely on standard techniques like equation merging, but the very last one uses a relatively new method called “canonical representatives”.

2
Branching Reactive Bisimilarity

A
labelled transition system
(LTS) is a triple
(\mathbb{P},Act,\rightarrow)
with
\mathbb{P}
a set (of
states
or
processes
),
Act
a set (of
actions
) and
{\rightarrow}\in\mathbb{P}\times Act\times\mathbb{P}
. In this paper we consider LTSs with
Act:=A\uplus\{\tau,{\rm t}\}
, where
A
is a set of
visible actions
,
\tau
is the
hidden or invisible action
, and
{\rm t}
the
time-out action
. Let
A_{\tau}:=A\cup\{\tau\}
.
P\xrightarrow{\alpha}P^{\prime}
stands for
(P,\alpha,P^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
and these triplets are called
transitions
. Moreover,
P\xrightarrow{\mbox{\tiny\rm(}\alpha\mbox{\tiny\rm)}}P^{\prime}
denotes that either
\alpha=\tau
and
P=P^{\prime}
, or
P\xrightarrow{\alpha}P^{\prime}
. Furthermore,
paths
are sequences of connected transitions and
\Longrightarrow
is the reflexive-transitive closure of
\xrightarrow{\tau}
. The set of
initial
actions of a process
P\in\mathbb{P}
is
\mathcal{I}(P):=\{\alpha\in A_{\tau}\mid P{\xrightarrow{\alpha}}\}
. Here
P{\xrightarrow{\alpha}}
means that there is a
Q
with
P\xrightarrow{\alpha}Q
.

Definition 1
.

A
concrete branching reactive bisimulation
is a symmetric
1
1

1

meaning that
(P,Q)\in\R\Leftrightarrow(Q,P)\in\R
and
(P,X,Q)\in\R\Leftrightarrow(Q,X,P)\in\R
relation
\R\subseteq(\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P})\cup(\mathbb{P}\times\Pow(A)\times\mathbb{P})
such that, for all
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}
and
X\subseteq A
,

1.

if
\R(P,Q)
then

(a)

if
P\xrightarrow{\alpha}P^{\prime}
with
\alpha\in A_{\tau}
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{1}\xrightarrow{\mbox{\tiny\rm(}\alpha\mbox{\tiny\rm)}}Q_{2}
with
\R(P,Q_{1})
and
\R(P^{\prime},Q_{2})
,

(b)

for all
Y\subseteq A
,
\R(P,Y,Q)
;

2.

if
\R(P,X,Q)
then

(a)

if
P\xrightarrow{\tau}P^{\prime}
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{1}\xrightarrow{\mbox{\tiny\rm(}\tau\mbox{\tiny\rm)}}Q_{2}
with
\R(P,X,Q_{1})
and
\R(P^{\prime},X,Q_{2})
,

(b)

if
P\xrightarrow{a}P^{\prime}
with
a\in X
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{1}\xrightarrow{a}Q_{2}
with
\R(P,X,Q_{1})
and
\R(P^{\prime},Q_{2})
,

(c)

if
\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{0}
with
\R(P,Q_{0})
,

(d)

if
\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
and
P\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}P^{\prime}
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{1}\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}Q_{2}
with
\R(P^{\prime},X,Q_{2})
,

(e)

if
P\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{0}\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
.

For
P,Q\mathbin{\in}\mathbb{P}
, if there exists a concrete branching reactive bisimulation
\R
with
\R(P,Q)
(resp.
\R(P,X,Q)
) then
P
and
Q
are said to be
concrete branching reactive bisimilar
(resp.
concrete branching
X
-bisimilar
), which is denoted
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q
(resp.
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cX}_{br}\!}Q
).

To build the above definition, the definition of a strong reactive bisimulation
[
11
]
was modified in a branching manner
[
13
]
. Intuitively, a triplet
\R(P,X,Q)
affirms that
P
and
Q
behave similarly when the environment allows (only) the set of actions in
X
to occur, whereas a couple
\R(P,Q)
says that
P
and
Q
behave in the same way when the environment has been triggered to change. As said before, the environment can be seen as a system executed in parallel while enforcing the synchronisation of all visible actions.

Clause 1 captures the scenario of a triggered environment: if
P
can perform a visible or invisible action then
Q
has to be able to match it; and the environment can settle on a set
Y
of allowed actions at any moment. Time-outs are not considered because these can occur only when the system idles, and idling can happen only when the environment has stabilised on a set of allowed actions. One might notice that, in
[
11
]
, the first clause was only required for invisible actions. However, there the case
\alpha\neq\tau
is actually implied by the other clauses. If in our definition Clause 1.a were restricted to invisible actions then
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
would not be a congruence for the parallel operator, as shown in Appendix
A
.

Clause 2 depicts the scenario of an environment allowing
X
.
\tau
-transitions have to be matched
since the environment cannot disallow them, and their execution does not trigger the environment to
change. Visible actions have to be matched only if they are allowed, and their execution triggers
the environment. Triggering the environment or not explains why Clause 2a matches
Q_{2}
in a triplet
and Clause 2b in a couple. If
P
idles (i.e.
\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
) then the environment can be
triggered, thus,
Q
has to be able to instantaneously reach a state
Q_{0}
related to
P
in a
triggered environment.
2
2

2

By Lemma
2
.4 we can even choose
Q_{0}
such that
Q_{0}\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
, so that
\mathcal{I}(Q_{0})=\mathcal{I}(P)
.
If
P
idles and has an outgoing time-out transition then
Q
has to be able to match it in a branching manner. Unlike the branching reactive bisimulation, this bisimulation does not allow to elide time-out transitions, thus, the matching resembles the one with visible action except that the execution of a time-out transition does not trigger the environment.
3
3

3

It is not necessary to match
P
and
Q_{1}
as it is implied by other clauses (see Lemma
2
.1), nor to require that
\mathcal{I}(Q_{1})\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
because it is also implied by the other clauses (see Lemma
2
.4).

Lastly, a stability respecting clause
[
7
]
was added for practical reasons. In Appendix
A
, an example shows that without it
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
would not even be an equivalence. For the important class of
divergence-free
systems, without infinite sequences
Q_{0}\xrightarrow{\tau}Q_{1}\xrightarrow{\tau}\dots
, Clause 2.e is easily seen to be redundant.

Lemma 2
.

Let
\R
be a concrete branching reactive bisimulation.

1.

If
\R(P,X,Q)
,
P\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
and
Q\Longrightarrow Q^{\prime}
then also
\R(P,X,Q^{\prime})
.

2.

If
\R(P,Q)
or
\R(P,X,Q)
,
P\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
and
Q\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
then
\mathcal{I}(Q)=\mathcal{I}(P)
.

3.

If
\R(P,X,Q)
,
\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
and
Q\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
then
\R(P,Q)
.

4.

If
\R(P,X,Q)
and
\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{0}
with
\R(P,Q_{0})
,
Q_{0}\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
and
\mathcal{I}(Q_{0})=\mathcal{I}(P)
.

Proof.

1.

This is an immediate consequence of the symmetric counterpart of Clause 2.a (where
Q
takes a
\tau
-step).
When that clause yields
P\Longrightarrow P_{1}\xrightarrow{\mbox{\tiny\rm(}\tau\mbox{\tiny\rm)}}P_{2}
we have
P_{2}=P
.

2.

This is a direct consequence of Clause 1.a or 2.b and its symmetric counterpart.

3.

By Clause 2.e there is path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{0}
with
Q_{0}\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
. By Claim 1 of this lemma,
\R(P,X,Q_{0})
. Thus, by Clause 2.c there is a path
Q_{0}\mathbin{\Longrightarrow}Q_{1}
with
\R(\hskip-1.0ptP,\hskip-1.0ptQ_{1}\hskip-1.0pt)
, but
Q_{1}\mathop{=}Q_{0}\mathop{=}Q
since
Q\!\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
.

4.

By Clause 2.e there is path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{0}
with
Q_{0}\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
. By Claim 1 of this lemma,
\R(P,X,Q_{0})
. That
\mathcal{I}(Q_{0})=\mathcal{I}(P)
and
\R(P,Q_{0})
follows by Claims 2 and 3 of this lemma.
∎

In
[
13
]
, branching bisimilarity is expressed in multiple equivalent ways. For practical purposes, our definition uses the semi-branching format, which is equivalent to the branching format thanks to the following lemma.

Lemma 3

(Stuttering Lemma)
.

Let
P,P^{\dagger},P^{\ddagger},Q\in\mathbb{P}
, if
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q
,
P^{\ddagger}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q
(resp.
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cX}_{br}\!}Q
,
P^{\ddagger}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cX}_{br}\!}Q
) and
P\xrightarrow{\tau}P^{\dagger}\xrightarrow{\tau}P^{\ddagger}
then
P^{\dagger}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q
(resp.
P^{\dagger}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cX}_{br}\!}Q
).

Proof.

Let
\R
be a concrete branching reactive bisimulation. Let’s define
\R^{\prime}:=\R\cup\{(P^{\dagger},Q),(Q,P^{\dagger})\mid\exists P,P^{\ddagger}\in\mathbb{P},P\Longrightarrow P^{\dagger}\Longrightarrow P^{\ddagger}\wedge\R(P,Q)\wedge\R(P^{\ddagger},Q)\}\cup\{(P^{\dagger},X,Q),\linebreak(Q,X,P^{\dagger})\mid\exists P,P^{\ddagger}\mathbin{\in}\mathbb{P},P\Longrightarrow P^{\dagger}\Longrightarrow P^{\ddagger}\wedge\R(P,X,Q)\wedge\R(P^{\ddagger},X,Q)\}
.
\R^{\prime}
is symmetric by definition and
\R^{\prime}
is a concrete branching reactive bisimulation, as proven in Appendix
D
.
∎

Proposition 4
.

\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
and
(\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cX}_{br}\!})_{X\subseteq A}
are equivalence relations.

Proof.

Reflexivity and symmetry are trivial following the definition. For transitivity, consider two concrete branching reactive bisimulations
\R_{1}
and
\R_{2}
. Let’s define
\R:=(\R_{1}\circ\R_{2})\cup(\R_{2}\circ\R_{1})
. Here
\R_{1}\circ\R_{2}:=\{(P,Q)\mid\exists R.~\R(P,R)\wedge\R(R,Q)\}\cup\{(P,X,Q)\mid\exists R.~\R(P,X,R)\wedge\R(R,X,Q)\}
.
\R
is symmetric by definition and
\R
is a concrete branching reactive bisimulation, as proven in Appendix
D
.
∎

2.1
Rooted Version

A well-known limitation of branching bisimilarity
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{s}_{b}\!}
is that it fails to be a congruence for the choice operator
+
. For example,
a\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{s}_{b}\!}\tau.a
but
a+b\,\not\!\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{s}_{b}\!}\tau.a+b
. Since the objective is to define a congruence, instead of
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
we use the
congruence closure
of
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
, which is the coarsest congruence included in
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
.

Definition 5
.

A
rooted concrete branching reactive bisimulation
is a symmetric relation
\R\subseteq(\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P})\cup(\mathbb{P}\times\Pow(A)\times\mathbb{P})
such that, for all
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}
and
X\subseteq A
,

1.

if
\R(P,Q)

(a)

if
P\xrightarrow{\alpha}P^{\prime}
with
\alpha\in A_{\tau}
then there is a transition
Q\xrightarrow{\alpha}Q^{\prime}
with
P^{\prime}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q^{\prime}
,

(b)

for all
Y\subseteq A
,
\R(P,Y,Q)
;

2.

if
\R(P,X,Q)

(a)

if
P\xrightarrow{\tau}P^{\prime}
then there is a transition
Q\xrightarrow{\tau}Q^{\prime}
with
P^{\prime}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cX}_{br}\!}Q^{\prime}
,

(b)

if
P\xrightarrow{a}P^{\prime}
with
a\in X
then there is a transition
Q\xrightarrow{a}Q^{\prime}
with
P^{\prime}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q^{\prime}
,

(c)

if
\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
then
\R(P,Q)
,

(d)

if
\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
and
P\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}P^{\prime}
then there is a transition
Q\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}Q^{\prime}
with
P^{\prime}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cX}_{br}\!}Q^{\prime}
.

For
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}
, if there exists a rooted concrete branching reactive bisimulation
\R
with
\R(P,Q)
(resp.
\R(P,X,Q)
) then
P
and
Q
are said to be
rooted concrete branching reactive bisimilar
(resp. rooted concrete branching
X
-bisimilar), which is denoted
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}Q
(resp.
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{crX}_{br}\!}Q
).

A rooted version of a bisimulation consists in enforcing a stricter matching on the first transition of a system. In the branching case, the first transition is matched in the strong manner. The stability respecting clause can be removed, as it is now implied by the other clauses. Rooting the bisimilarity is the standard technique to obtain its congruence closure; later
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
will be proven to be a congruence. As any concrete branching reactive bisimulation relating
P+b
and
Q+b
, for a fresh action
b
, induces a rooted concrete branching reactive bisimulation relating
P
and
Q
, it then follows that
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
is the coarsest congruence included in
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
. Since
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
is an equivalence, the proof of Proposition
4
can be adapted to
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
in a straightforward way.

Proposition 6
.

\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
and
(\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{crX}_{br}\!})_{X\subseteq A}
are equivalence relations.

2.2
Alternative Forms of Definition
1

Definition
1
can be rephrased in various ways. First of all, using Requirements 1.b and 2.c, one can move Requirement 2.d from Clause 2 (dealing with triples
(P,X,Q)
) to Clause 1 (dealing with pairs
(P,Q)
), now adding a universal quantifier over
X
to the requirement. Next, Requirement 2.e can be copied under Clause 1. This makes Clause 1.b unnecessary, thereby obtaining a definition in which the triples
(P,X,Q)
are encountered only after taking a
{\rm t}
-transition. In this form it is obvious that concrete branching reactive bisimilarity reduces to the classical stability respecting branching bisimilarity for systems without
{\rm t}
-transitions. We have chosen the form of Definition
1
over the above alternatives, because we believe it comes with more natural intuitions for its plausibility.

In Appendix
B
a further modification of Definitions
1
and
5
is proposed, called
generalised [rooted] concrete branching reactive bisimulation
. We show that each [rooted] concrete branching reactive bisimulation is a generalised [rooted] concrete branching reactive bisimulation, and two systems are [rooted] concrete branching reactive bisimilar iff they are related by a generalised [rooted] concrete branching reactive bisimulation.
This characterisation of
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
and
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
will be used in the proofs of Theorem
11
and Proposition
15
.

In
[
19
]
, Pohlmann introduces an encoding which maps strong reactive bisimilarity to strong bisimilarity where time-outs are considered as any visible action. This encoding in essence places a given process in a most general environment, one that features environment time-out actions
{\rm t}_{\varepsilon}
, as well as actions
\varepsilon_{X}
for settling in a state that allows exactly the actions in
X
. This proves that reactive equivalences can be expressed as non-reactive ones at the cost of increasing the processes’ size. Thus, any tool set able to work on strong bisimulation could theoretically deal with its reactive counterpart.

In Appendix
C
, this encoding is slightly modified to yield a similar result for concrete branching reactive bisimulation and its rooted version, for the latter result also employing actions
{\rm t}_{X}
. It appears that these modifications do not impact its effect on strong reactive bisimilarity. This encoding maps our bisimilarity to the traditional stability respecting branching bisimilarity and
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
to rooted stability respecting branching bisimilarity
[
7
,
5
]
.

Definition 7
.

A
stability respecting branching bisimulation
is a symmetric relation
\R\subseteq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}
such that, for all
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}
, if
\R(P,Q)
then

1.

if
P\xrightarrow{\alpha}P^{\prime}
with
\alpha\in Act\cup\{{\rm t}_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon_{X}\mid X\subseteq A\}
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{1}\xrightarrow{\mbox{\tiny\rm(}\alpha\mbox{\tiny\rm)}}Q_{2}
with
\R(P,Q_{1})
and
\R(P^{\prime},Q_{2})
,

2.

if
P\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{0}\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
.

For
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}
, if there exists a stability respecting branching bisimulation
\R
with
\R(P,Q)
then
P
and
Q
are said to be
stability respecting branching bisimilar
, which is denoted
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{s}_{b}\!}Q
.

Definition 8
.

A
rooted stability respecting branching bisimulation
is a symmetric relation
\R\subseteq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}
such that, for all
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}
, if
\R(P,Q)
then

1.

if
P\mathord{\xrightarrow{\alpha}}P^{\prime}
with
\alpha\mathord{\in}Act\mathop{\cup}\{{\rm t}_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon_{X}\mathbin{\mid}X\mathord{\subseteq}A\}
then there is a transition
Q\mathop{\xrightarrow{\alpha}}Q^{\prime}
with
P^{\prime}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{s}_{b}\!}Q^{\prime}\!
.

For
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}
, if there exists a rooted stability respecting branching bisimulation
\R
with
\R(P,Q)
then
P
and
Q
are said to be
rooted stability respecting branching bisimilar
, which is denoted
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}Q
.

3
Modal Characterisation

The Hennessy-Milner logic
[
15
]
expresses properties of the behaviour of processes in an LTS. In
[
11
]
, the modality
\langle X\rangle\varphi
was added to obtain a modal characterisation of strong reactive bisimilarity (
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,_{r}\!}
).

Definition 9
.

The class
\mathbb{L}
of
reactive Hennessy-Milner formulas
is defined as follows, where
I
is an index set,
\alpha\in A_{\tau}
,
a\in A
and
X\subseteq A
,

\varphi::=\top\mid\bigwedge\limits_{i\in I}\varphi_{i}\mid\neg\varphi\mid\langle\alpha\rangle\varphi\mid\langle X\rangle\varphi

P\models\top

P\models_{Y}\top

P\models\bigwedge_{i\in I}\varphi_{i}

iff

\forall i\in I,P\models\varphi_{i}

P\models_{Y}\bigwedge_{i\in I}\varphi_{i}

iff

\forall i\in I,P\models_{Y}\varphi_{i}

P\models\neg\varphi

iff

P\not\models\varphi

P\models_{Y}\neg\varphi

iff

P\not\models_{Y}\varphi

P\models\langle\alpha\rangle\varphi

iff

\exists P\xrightarrow{\alpha}P^{\prime},P^{\prime}\models\varphi

P\models_{Y}\langle\tau\rangle\varphi

iff

\exists P\xrightarrow{\tau}P^{\prime},P^{\prime}\models_{Y}\varphi

P\models_{Y}\langle a\rangle\varphi

iff

(a\in Y\vee\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(Y\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset)\wedge\exists P\xrightarrow{a}P^{\prime},P^{\prime}\models\varphi

P\models\langle X\rangle\varphi

iff

\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset\wedge\exists P\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}P^{\prime},P^{\prime}\models_{X}\varphi

P\models_{Y}\langle X\rangle\varphi

iff

\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(X\cup Y\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset\wedge\exists P\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}P^{\prime},P^{\prime}\models_{X}\varphi

Table 1:
Semantics of
\models
and
(\models_{Y})_{Y\subseteq A}

The satisfaction rules of
\mathbb{L}
are given in Table
1
.
P\models\varphi
means that
P
satisfies
\varphi
when the environment is triggered, and
P\models_{Y}\varphi
indicates that
P
satisfies
\varphi
when the environment allows
Y
. The modality
\langle X\rangle\varphi
expresses that a process can idle in its current state during a period in which the environment allows the actions in
X
and from which it can perform a time-out transition to a state which satisfies
\varphi
while the environment keeps allowing
X
. The definition above captures that
P\models_{Y}\varphi
whenever
\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(Y\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
and
P\models\varphi
. This is because the environment may choose to change during a period of idling.
The modal characterisation theorem of
[
11
]
says
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,_{r}\!}Q~~\Leftrightarrow~~\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{L}.~(P\models\varphi\Leftrightarrow Q\models\varphi)\;.

To obtain a modal characterisation of [rooted] concrete branching relative bisimilarity, we need a few other derived modalities. First of all,
\langle\varepsilon\rangle\varphi:=\bigvee_{i\in{\rm Nature}}\langle\tau\rangle^{i}\varphi
. To lessen the notations, for all
\alpha\in A_{\tau}
,
\langle\hat{\alpha}\rangle\varphi
denotes
\varphi\vee\langle\tau\rangle\varphi
if
\alpha=\tau
,
\langle\alpha\rangle\varphi
otherwise. Moreover,
\varphi\wedge\langle\hat{\alpha}\rangle\varphi^{\prime}
is shortened to
\varphi\langle\hat{\alpha}\rangle\varphi^{\prime}
.
The satisfaction rules of these new modalities can be derived from the basic ones: see Table
2
.

P\models\langle\hat{\alpha}\rangle\varphi

iff

\exists P\xrightarrow{\mbox{\tiny\rm(}\alpha\mbox{\tiny\rm)}}P^{\prime},P^{\prime}\models\varphi

P\models_{Y}\langle\hat{\tau}\rangle\varphi

iff

\exists P\xrightarrow{\mbox{\tiny\rm(}\tau\mbox{\tiny\rm)}}P^{\prime},P^{\prime}\models_{Y}\varphi

P\models\langle\varepsilon\rangle\varphi

iff

\exists P\Longrightarrow P^{\prime},P^{\prime}\models\varphi

P\models_{Y}\langle\varepsilon\rangle\varphi

iff

\exists P\Longrightarrow P^{\prime},P^{\prime}\models_{Y}\varphi

Table 2:
Semantics of
\models
and
(\models_{Y})_{Y\subseteq A}
for the derived modalities

Definition 10
.

The sub-classes
\mathbb{L}_{b}^{c}
and
\mathbb{L}_{b}^{cr}
are defined as follows, where
I
is an index set,
\alpha\in A_{\tau}
,
X\subseteq A
,
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{L}_{b}^{c}
and
\psi\in\mathbb{L}_{b}^{cr}
,

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle::=\top\mid\bigwedge_{i\in I}\varphi_{i}\mid\neg\varphi\mid\langle\varepsilon\rangle(\varphi\langle\hat{\alpha}\rangle\varphi^{\prime})\mid\langle\varepsilon\rangle\langle X\rangle\varphi^{\prime}\mid\langle\varepsilon\rangle\neg\langle\tau\rangle\top

(
\mathbb{L}_{b}^{c}
)

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle::=\top\mid\bigwedge_{i\in I}\psi_{i}\mid\neg\psi\mid\langle\alpha\rangle\varphi\mid\langle X\rangle\varphi

(
\mathbb{L}_{b}^{cr}
)

The last option for
\mathbb{L}_{b}^{c}
, inspired by
[
5
]
, is used to encompass the stability respecting Clause 2.e of Definition
1
.

Theorem 11
.

Let
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}
. For all
X\subseteq A
,

•

P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q
iff
\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{L}_{b}^{c},P\models\varphi\Leftrightarrow Q\models\varphi
,

•

P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cX}_{br}\!}Q
iff
\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{L}_{b}^{c},P\models_{X}\varphi\Leftrightarrow Q\models_{X}\varphi
,

•

P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}Q
iff
\forall\psi\in\mathbb{L}_{b}^{cr},P\models\psi\Leftrightarrow Q\models\psi
,

•

P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{crX}_{br}\!}Q
iff
\forall\psi\in\mathbb{L}_{b}^{cr},P\models_{X}\psi\Leftrightarrow Q\models_{X}\psi
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
The four propositions are proven simultaneously by structural induction on
\mathbb{L}_{b}^{c}
and
\mathbb{L}_{b}^{cr}
in Appendix
E
.

(\Leftarrow)
Let
\equiv\;:=\{(P,Q)\mid\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{L}_{b}^{c},P\models\varphi\Leftrightarrow Q\models\varphi\}\cup\{(P,X,Q)\mid\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{L}_{b}^{c},P\models_{X}\varphi\Leftrightarrow Q\models_{X}\varphi\}
, and
\equiv^{r}\;:=\{(P,Q)\mid\forall\psi\in\mathbb{L}_{b}^{cr},P\models\psi\Leftrightarrow Q\models\psi\}\cup\{(P,X,Q)\mid\forall\psi\in\mathbb{L}_{b}^{cr},P\models_{X}\psi\Leftrightarrow Q\models_{X}\psi\}
. It suffices to check that
\equiv
[resp.
\equiv^{r}
] is a generalised [rooted] concrete branching reactive bisimulation. This is done in Appendix
E
.
∎

4
Process Algebra and Congruence

The process algebra
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
is composed of classical operators from the well-known process algebras CCS
[
17
]
, CSP
[
2
,
18
]
and ACP
[
1
,
4
]
, as well as the time-out action
{\rm t}
and two
environment operators
from
[
11
]
, that were added in order to enable a complete axiomatisation.

Definition 12
.

Let
V
be a countable set of variables, the
expressions
of
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
are recursively defined as follows:

\displaystyle E::=0\mid x\mid\alpha.E\mid E+F\mid E\parallel_{S}F\mid\tau_{I}(E)\mid\mathcal{R}(E)\mid\theta_{L}^{U}(E)\mid\psi_{X}(E)\mid\langle y|\mathcal{S}\rangle

where
x\in V
,
\alpha\in Act
,
S,I,L,U,X\subseteq A
,
L\subseteq U
,
\mathcal{R}\subseteq A\times A
,
\mathcal{S}
is a
recursive specification
: a set of equations
\{x\mathbin{=}\mathcal{S}_{x}\mid x\mathbin{\in}V_{\mathcal{S}}\}
with
V_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq V
and each
\mathcal{S}_{x}
a
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
expression, and
y\mathbin{\in}V_{\mathcal{S}}
. We require that all sets
{\{b\mid(a,b)\in\mathcal{R}\}}
are finite.

0
stands for a system which cannot perform any action. The expression
\alpha.E
represents a system that first performs
\alpha
and then
E
. The expression
E+F
represents a choice to behave like
E
or
F
. The parallel composition
E\parallel_{S}F
synchronises the execution of
E
and
F
, but only when performing actions in
S
.
\tau_{I}(E)
represents the system
E
where all actions
a\mathbin{\in}I
are transformed into
\tau
. The operator
\mathcal{R}
renames a given action
a\mathbin{\in}A
into a choice between all actions
b
with
(a,b)\mathbin{\in}\mathcal{R}
.
\langle y|\mathcal{S}\rangle
is the
y
-component of a solution of
\mathcal{S}
.

\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
also has two environment operators that help to develop a complete axiomatisation (like the left merge for ACP).
\theta_{L}^{U}(E)
is the expression
E
plunged into an environment
X
such that
L\subseteq X\subseteq U
.
\theta_{X}^{X}(E)
is denoted
\theta_{X}(E)
.
\psi_{X}(E)
plunges
E
into the environment
X
if a time-out occurs, but, has no effect if any other action is performed. The operational semantics of
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
is given in Figure
1
. All operators except the environment ones follow the semantics of CCS, CSP or ACP. As
\theta_{L}^{U}(E)
simulates the expression
E
plunged in an environment
L\subseteq X\subseteq U
, it has no effect on
\tau
-transitions, which do not trigger the environment. Moreover,
\theta_{L}^{U}
restricts the ability to perform visible actions to those allowed by the environment (i.e. included in
U
) and performing these actions triggers the environment. However, if the expression idles (i.e.
\mathcal{I}(E)\cap(L\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
) then it might trigger the environment and
\theta_{L}^{U}(E)
acts like
E
.
\psi_{X}(E)
supposes that time-outs are performed while the environment allows
X
, thus, it has no effect on actions that are not
{\rm t}
. However, if
E
can perform a time-out while the environment allows
X
(i.e.
\mathcal{I}(E)\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
) then
\psi_{X}(E)
can perform the time-out while plunging the expression in the environment
X
.

x\xrightarrow{\alpha}y

\alpha.x\xrightarrow{\alpha}x

x\xrightarrow{\alpha}x^{\prime}

x+y\xrightarrow{\alpha}x^{\prime}

y\xrightarrow{\alpha}y^{\prime}

x+y\xrightarrow{\alpha}y^{\prime}

x\xrightarrow{a}x^{\prime}\wedge\mathcal{R}(a,b)

\mathcal{R}(x)\xrightarrow{b}\mathcal{R}(x^{\prime})

x\xrightarrow{\tau}x^{\prime}

\mathcal{R}(x)\xrightarrow{\tau}\mathcal{R}(x^{\prime})

x\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}x^{\prime}

\mathcal{R}(x)\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}\mathcal{R}(x^{\prime})

x\xrightarrow{\alpha}x^{\prime}\wedge\alpha\not\in S

x\parallel_{S}y\xrightarrow{\alpha}x^{\prime}\parallel_{S}y

y\xrightarrow{\alpha}y^{\prime}\wedge\alpha\not\in S

x\parallel_{S}y\xrightarrow{\alpha}x\parallel_{S}y^{\prime}

x\xrightarrow{a}x^{\prime}\wedge y\xrightarrow{a}y^{\prime}\wedge a\in S

x\parallel_{S}y\xrightarrow{a}x^{\prime}\parallel_{S}y^{\prime}

x\xrightarrow{\alpha}x^{\prime}\wedge\alpha\not\in I

\tau_{I}(x)\xrightarrow{\alpha}\tau_{I}(x^{\prime})

x\xrightarrow{a}x^{\prime}\wedge a\in I

\tau_{I}(x)\xrightarrow{\tau}\tau_{I}(x^{\prime})

\langle\mathcal{S}_{x}|\mathcal{S}\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}x^{\prime}

\langle x|\mathcal{S}\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha}x^{\prime}

x\xrightarrow{\tau}x^{\prime}

\theta_{L}^{U}(x)\xrightarrow{\tau}\theta_{L}^{U}(x^{\prime})

x\xrightarrow{a}x^{\prime}\wedge a\in U

\theta_{L}^{U}(x)\xrightarrow{a}x^{\prime}

x\xrightarrow{\alpha}x^{\prime}\wedge\alpha\neq t

\psi_{X}(x)\xrightarrow{\alpha}x^{\prime}

x\xrightarrow{\alpha}x^{\prime}\wedge\mathcal{I}(x)\cap(L\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset

\theta_{L}^{U}(x)\xrightarrow{\alpha}x^{\prime}

x\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}x^{\prime}\wedge\mathcal{I}(x)\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset

\psi_{X}(x)\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}\theta_{X}(x^{\prime})

Figure 1:
Operational semantics of
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}

All
\mathcal{S}_{x}
are considered to be sub-expressions of
\langle y|\mathcal{S}\rangle
. An occurrence of a variable
x
is
bound
in
E\in\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
iff it occurs in a sub-expression
\langle y|\mathcal{S}\rangle
of
E
such that
x\in V_{\mathcal{S}}
; otherwise it is
free
. An expression
E
is
invalid
if it has a sub-expression
\theta_{L}^{U}(F)
or
\psi_{X}(F)
such that a variable occurrence is free in
F
, but bound in
E
. An example justifying this condition can be found in
[
11
]
. The set of valid expressions of
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
is denoted
\mathbb{E}
. If an expression is valid and all of its variable occurrences are bound then it is
closed
and we call it a
process
; the set of processes is denoted
\mathbb{P}
.

A
substitution
is a partial function
\rho:V\rightharpoonup E
. The application
E[\rho]
of a substitution
\rho
to an expression
E\in\mathbb{E}
is the result of the simultaneous replacement, for all
x\in\mbox{dom}(\rho)
, of each free occurrence of
x
by the expression
\rho(x)
, while renaming bound variables to avoid name clashes. We write
\langle E|\mathcal{S}\rangle
for the expression
E
where any
y\in V_{\mathcal{S}}
is substituted by
\langle y|\mathcal{S}\rangle
.

4.1
Time-out Bisimulation

Thanks to the environment operator
\theta_{L}^{U}
, it is possible to express our bisimilarity in a much more succinct way. Indeed,
\theta_{X}
was defined so that
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cX}_{br}\!}Q
if and only if
\theta_{X}(P)\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}\theta_{X}(Q)
.

Definition 13
.

A
concrete branching time-out bisimulation
is a symmetric relation
{\B}\subseteq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}
such that, for all
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}
, if
P\B Q
then

1.

if
P\xrightarrow{\alpha}P^{\prime}
with
\alpha\in A_{\tau}
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{1}\xrightarrow{\mbox{\tiny\rm(}\alpha\mbox{\tiny\rm)}}Q_{2}
with
P\B Q_{1}
and
P^{\prime}\B Q_{2}

2.

if
\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
and
P\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}P^{\prime}
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{1}\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}Q_{2}
with
\theta_{X}(P^{\prime})\B\theta_{X}(Q_{2})

3.

if
P\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{0}\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}
.

Note that in Condition 2 above one also has
P\B Q_{1}
and consequently
\mathcal{I}(Q_{1})\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
. A rooted version of concrete branching time-out bisimulation can be defined in the same vein.

Definition 14
.

A
rooted concrete branching time-out bisimulation
is a symmetric relation
{\B}\subseteq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}
such that, for all
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}
such that
P\B Q
,

1.

if
P\xrightarrow{\alpha}P^{\prime}
with
\alpha\in A_{\tau}
then there is a step
Q\xrightarrow{\alpha}Q^{\prime}
such that
P^{\prime}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q^{\prime}

2.

if
\mathcal{I}(P)\cap(X\cup\{\tau\})\mathbin{=}\emptyset
and
P\mathbin{\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}}P^{\prime}
then there is a step
Q\mathbin{\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}}Q^{\prime}
such that
\theta_{X}(P^{\prime})\mathbin{\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}}\theta_{X}(Q^{\prime}).

Proposition 15
.

Let
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}
,

1.

P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q
(resp.
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cX}_{br}\!}Q
) iff there exists a concrete branching time-out bisimulation
\B
with
P\B Q
(resp.
(\theta_{X}(P)\B\theta_{X}(Q)
),

2.

P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cX}_{br}\!}Q
if and only if
\theta_{X}(P)\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}\theta_{X}(Q)
,

3.

P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}Q
(resp.
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{crX}_{br}\!}Q
) iff there exists a rooted concrete branching time-out bisimulation
\B
with
P\B Q
(resp.
(\theta_{X}(P)\B\theta_{X}(Q)
).

Proof.

Note that Proposition
15
.
2
is a trivial corollary of
15
.1.

Let
\R
be a [generalised rooted] concrete branching reactive bisimulation, let’s define
{\B}:=\{(P,Q)\mid\R(P,Q)\}\cup\{(\theta_{X}(P),\theta_{X}(Q))\mid\R(P,X,Q)\}
.
\B
is a [rooted] concrete branching time-out bisimulation, as proven in Appendix
F
. Let
\B
be a [rooted] concrete branching time-out bisimulation, let’s define
\R=\{(P,Q)\mid P\B Q\}\cup\{(P,X,Q)\mid\theta_{X}(P)\B\theta_{X}(Q)\}
.
\R
is a [rooted] generalised concrete branching reactive bisimulation, as proven in Appendix
F
.
∎

Time-out bisimulations are very practical as there are no triplets to deal with anymore.

4.2
Congruence

Until now, bisimilarity was only defined between closed expressions, but any relation
{\sim}\subseteq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}
can be extended to
\mathbb{E}\times\mathbb{E}
in the following way:
E\sim F
iff
\forall\rho:V\rightarrow\mathbb{P},\;E[\rho]\sim F[\rho]
. It can be extended further to substitutions
\rho,\nu\in V\rightharpoonup\mathbb{E}
by
\rho\sim\nu
iff
\mbox{dom}(\rho)=\mbox{dom}(\nu)
and
\forall x\in\mbox{dom}(\rho),\;\rho(x)\sim\nu(x)
.

Definition 16
.

An equivalence
{\sim}\subseteq\mathbb{E}\times\mathbb{E}
is a congruence for an
n
-ary operator
f
if
P_{i}\sim Q_{i}
for all
i=0,\dots,n{-}1
implies
f(P_{0},...,P_{n-1})\sim f(Q_{0},...,Q_{n-1})
. It is a
lean congruence
if, for all
E\in\mathbb{E}
and all
\rho,\nu\in V\rightharpoonup\mathbb{E}
such that
\rho\sim\nu
,
E[\rho]\sim E[\nu]
. It is a
full congruence
if

1.

it is a congruence for all operators in the language, and

2.

for all recursive specifications
\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
with
V_{\mathcal{S}}=V_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}
and
x\in V_{\mathcal{S}}
such that
\langle x|\mathcal{S}\rangle,\langle x|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\rangle\in\mathbb{P}
, if
\forall y\in V_{\mathcal{S}},\;\mathcal{S}_{y}\sim\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{y}
then
\langle x|\mathcal{S}\rangle\sim\langle x|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\rangle
.

To show that
\sim
is a lean congruence it suffices to restrict attention to closed substitutions
\rho,\nu\in V\rightarrow\mathbb{P}
, because the general property will then follow by composition of substitutions. A full congruence is a lean congruence, and a lean congruence is a congruence for all operators in the language, but both implications are strict, as shown in
[
8
]
.

To show that
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
and
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}
are full congruences, it is first necessary to prove that
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
and
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{s}_{b}\!}
are congruences for some of the operators of
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
.

Proposition 17
.

\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
and
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{s}_{b}\!}
are congruences for action prefixing, parallel composition, abstraction, renaming and the environment operator
\theta_{L}^{U}
, for all
L\subseteq U\subseteq A
.

Proof.

Let
\B
be the smallest relation such that, for all
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}
,

•

if
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q
then
P\B Q
;

•

if
P\B Q
then, for all
\alpha\in Act
,
I\subseteq A
,
\mathcal{R}\in A\times A
and
L\subseteq U\subseteq A
,
\alpha.P\B\alpha.Q
,
\tau_{I}(P)\B\tau_{I}(Q)
,
\mathcal{R}(P)\B\mathcal{R}(Q)
and
\theta_{L}^{U}(P)\B\theta_{L}^{U}(Q)
;

•

if
P_{1}\B Q_{1}
,
P_{2}\B Q_{2}
and
S\subseteq A
then
P_{1}\parallel_{S}P_{2}\B Q_{1}\parallel_{S}Q_{2}
.

It suffices to show that
\B
is a concrete branching time-out bisimulation up to
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,\!}
, which implies
{\B}\subseteq{\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}}\,
. A bisimulation “up to” is a notion introduced by Milner in
[
17
]
; it is commonly used when proving congruence properties. The proof uses some lemmas which were obtained in
[
11
]
. Details can be found in Appendix
G
. A similar proof yields the result for
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{s}_{b}\!}
. ∎

Theorem 18
.

\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
and
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}
are full congruences.

Proof.

Let
{\B}\subseteq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}
be the smallest relation such that

•

if
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}Q
then
P\B Q
;

•

if
P_{1}\B Q_{1}
and
P_{2}\B Q_{2}
then
P_{1}+P_{2}\B Q_{1}+Q_{2}
and
\forall S\subseteq A,\;P_{1}\parallel_{S}P_{2}\B Q_{1}\parallel_{S}Q_{2}
;

•

if
P\B Q
then
\forall\alpha\mathbin{\in}Act,\;\alpha.P\B\alpha.Q
,
\forall I\subseteq A,\;\tau_{I}(P)\B\tau_{I}(Q)
,
\forall\mathcal{R}\subseteq A\times A,\;\mathcal{R}(P)\B\mathcal{R}(Q)
,
\forall L\mathbin{\subseteq}U\mathbin{\subseteq}A,\;\theta_{L}^{U}(P)\B\theta_{L}^{U}(Q)
and
\forall X\subseteq A,\;\psi_{X}(P)\B\psi_{X}(Q)
;

•

if
\mathcal{S}
is a recursive specification with
z\in V_{\mathcal{S}}
and
\rho,\nu\in V\setminus V_{\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}
are substitutions such that
\forall x\in V\setminus V_{\mathcal{S}},\;\rho(x)\B\nu(x)
, then
\langle z|\mathcal{S}\rangle[\rho]\B\langle z|\mathcal{S}\rangle[\nu]
;

•

if
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
are recursive specifications and
x\in V_{\mathcal{S}}=V_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}
with
\langle x|\mathcal{S}\rangle,\langle x|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\rangle\in\mathbb{P}
such that
\forall y\in V_{\mathcal{S}},\;\mathcal{S}_{y}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{y}
, then
\langle x|\mathcal{S}\rangle\B\langle x|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\rangle
.

Since
{\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}}\subseteq{\B}
, it suffices to prove that
\B
is a rooted concrete branching time-out bisimulation up to
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
, as done in Appendix
H
. This implies
{\B}={\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}}
and the definition will then give us that
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
is a lean congruence. Moreover, the last condition of
\B
adds that it is a full congruence. A similar proof yields the result for
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}
.
∎

5
Axiomatisation

We will provide complete axiomatisations for
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
and
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}
on various fragments of
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
.

5.1
Recursive Principles

The expression
\langle x|\mathcal{S}\rangle
is intuitively defined as the
x
-component of the solution of
\mathcal{S}
. However,
\mathcal{S}
could perfectly well have multiple solutions that are not bisimilar to each other. For instance, take
\mathcal{S}=\{x\mathbin{=}x\}
; any expression is an
x
-component of a solution of
\mathcal{S}
. For our complete axiomatisation, we need to restrict attention to recursive specifications which have a unique solution with respect to our notion of bisimilarity. This property can be decomposed into two principles
[
1
,
4
]
: the
recursive definition principle
(RDP) states that a system of recursive equations has at least one solution and the
recursive specification principle
(RSP) that it has at most one solution. The latter holds under a condition traditionally called
guardedness
.

Definition 19
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a recursive specification and
{\sim}\subseteq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}
, a
solution up to
\sim
of
\mathcal{S}
is a substitution
\rho\in\mathbb{E}^{V_{\mathcal{S}}}
such that
\rho\sim\mathcal{S}[\rho]
. Here
\rho
and
{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathbb{E}^{V_{\mathcal{S}}}
are seen as
V_{\mathcal{S}}
-tuples.

In
[
1
,
4
]
RDP was proven for the classical notion of strong bisimilarity
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,\!}
. Since
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
and
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}
are included in
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,\!}
, it holds for both of these relations as well.

Proposition 20

(RDP)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a recursive specification. The substitution
\rho:x\mapsto\langle x|\mathcal{S}\rangle
for all
x\mathbin{\in}V_{\mathcal{S}}
is a solution of
\mathcal{S}
up to
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,\!}
. It is called the
default solution
of
\mathcal{S}
.

An occurrence of a variable
x
in an expression
E
is
well-guarded
if
x
occurs in a subexpression
a.F
of
E
, with
a\mathbin{\in}A\cup\{{\rm t}\}
. Here we do not allow
\tau
as a guard, but unlike in
[
20
]

{\rm t}
can be a guard. An expression
E
is
well-guarded
if no operator
\tau_{I}
occurs in
E
and all free occurrences of variables in
E
are well-guarded. A recursive specification
\mathcal{S}
is
manifestly well-guarded
if no operator
\tau_{I}
occurs in
\mathcal{S}
and for all
x,y\in V_{\mathcal{S}}
all occurrences of
x
in the expression
\mathcal{S}_{y}
are well-guarded; it is
well-guarded
if it can be made manifestly well-guarded by repeated substitution of
\mathcal{S}_{y}
for
y
within terms
\mathcal{S}_{x}
. A
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
process
P\in\mathbb{P}
is
guarded
if each recursive specification occurring in
E
is well-guarded. It is
strongly guarded
if moreover there is no infinite path of
\tau
-transitions starting in a state
P_{0}
reachable from
P\!
.

Proposition 21

(RSP)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a well-guarded recursive specification and
\rho,\nu\mathbin{\in}\mathbb{E}^{V_{\mathcal{S}}}\!\!
. If
\rho
and
\nu
are solutions of
\mathcal{S}
up to
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
(or
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}
) then
\rho\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}\nu
(resp.
\rho\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}\nu
).

Proof.

Modifying
\mathcal{S}
by substituting
\mathcal{S}_{y}
for
y
within terms
\mathcal{S}_{x}
with
x,y\in V_{\mathcal{S}}
does not affect the set of its solutions. Hence we can restrict attention to manifestly well-guarded
\mathcal{S}
.

Thanks to the composition of substitutions, it suffices to prove the proposition when
\rho,\nu\in\mathbb{P}^{V_{\mathcal{S}}}
and only variables of
V_{\mathcal{S}}
can occur in
\mathcal{S}_{x}
for
x\in V_{\mathcal{S}}
. It suffices to show that the symmetric closure of
{\B}:=\{(H[\mathcal{S}[\rho]],H[\mathcal{S}[\nu]])\mid H\in\mathbb{E}
is without
\tau_{I}
operators and with free variables from
V_{\mathcal{S}}\mbox{ only}\}
is a rooted concrete branching time-out bisimulation up to
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
. Here
\mathcal{S}[\rho]\in\mathbb{P}^{V_{\mathcal{S}}}
is seen as a substitution. Details can be found in Appendix
I
. An almost identical strategy can be applied to get RSP for
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}
.
∎

5.2
Axioms and Soundness

The set of axioms provided is composed of the axiomatisation of
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,\!}_{r}

[
11
]
, together with the
branching axiom
which is well-known since it is used in the axiomatisation of rooted branching bisimilarity
[
13
]
.

x+(y+z)=(x+y)+z

\tau_{I}(x+y)=\tau_{I}(x)+\tau_{I}(y)

\mathcal{R}(x+y)=\mathcal{R}(x)+\mathcal{R}(y)

x+y=y+x

\tau_{I}(\alpha.x)=\alpha.\tau_{I}(x)
if
\alpha\not\in I

\mathcal{R}(\tau.x)=\tau.\mathcal{R}(x)

x+x=0

\tau_{I}(\alpha.x)=\tau.\tau_{I}(x)
if
\alpha\in I

\mathcal{R}({\rm t}.x)={\rm t}.\mathcal{R}(x)

x+0=x

\mathcal{R}(a.x)=\sum_{\{b\mid\mathcal{R}(a,b)\}}b.\mathcal{R}(x)

Expansion Theorem:
if
P=\sum\limits_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}.P_{i}
and
Q=\sum\limits_{j\in J}\beta_{j}.Q_{j}
then

P\parallel_{S}Q=\sum\limits_{i\in I,\alpha_{i}\not\in S}(\alpha_{i}.P_{i}\parallel_{S}Q)+\sum\limits_{j\in J,\beta_{j}\not\in S}(P\parallel_{S}\beta_{j}.Q_{j})+\sum\limits_{i\in I,j\in J,\alpha_{i}=\beta_{j}\in S}\alpha_{i}.(P_{i}\parallel_{S}Q_{j})

\alpha.(\tau.(x+y)+x)=\alpha.(x+y)

(Branching Axiom)

\langle x|\mathcal{S}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{S}_{x}|\mathcal{S}\rangle

(RDP)

\mathcal{S}\Rightarrow x=\langle x|\mathcal{S}\rangle
with
\mathcal{S}
well-guarded
(RSP)

\theta_{L}^{U}(\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}.x_{i})=\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}.x_{i}

(\forall i\in I,\alpha_{i}\not\in L\cup\{\tau\})

\theta_{L}^{U}(x+\alpha.y+\beta.z)=\theta_{L}^{U}(x+\alpha.y)

(\alpha\in L\cup\{\tau\}\wedge\beta\not\in U\cup\{\tau\})

\theta_{L}^{U}(x+\alpha.y+\beta.z)=\theta^{U}_{L}(x+\alpha.y)+\theta_{L}^{U}(\beta.z)

(\alpha\in L\cup\{\tau\}\wedge\beta\in U\cup\{\tau\})

\theta_{L}^{U}(\alpha.x)=\alpha.x

(\alpha\neq\tau)

\theta_{L}^{U}(\tau.x)=\tau.\theta_{L}^{U}(x)

\psi_{X}(x+\alpha.y)=\psi_{X}(x)+\alpha.y

(\alpha\not\in X\cup\{\tau,t\})

\psi_{X}(x+\alpha.y+{\rm t}.z)=\psi_{X}(x+\alpha.y)

(\alpha\in X\cup\{\tau\})

\psi_{X}(x+\alpha.y+\beta.z)=\psi_{X}(x+\alpha.y)+\beta.z

(\alpha,\beta\in X\cup\{\tau\})

\psi_{X}(\alpha.x)=\alpha.x

(\alpha\neq{\rm t})

\psi_{X}(\sum_{i\in I}{\rm t}.y_{i})=\sum_{i\in I}{\rm t}.\theta_{X}(y_{i})

(\forall X\subseteq A,\;\psi_{X}(x)=\psi_{X}(y))\Rightarrow x=y

(Reactive Approximation Axiom)

Table 3:
Axiomatisation of
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
and
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}

Let
Ax^{\infty}
be the set of all axioms in the first two rectangles in Table
3
and
Ax:=Ax^{\infty}\setminus\{\mbox{RDP},\mbox{RSP}\}
. Let
Ax^{\infty}_{r}
be the set of all axioms in Table
3
and
Ax_{r}:=Ax^{\infty}_{r}\setminus\{\mbox{RDP},\mbox{RSP}\}
. The law
\mbox{\bf L}\tau
:
\tau.x+{\rm t}.y=\tau.x
can be derived from the reactive approximation axiom
[
11
]
.

Proposition 22
.

Let
P,Q
be two
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
processes.

•

If
Ax^{\infty}\vdash P=Q
then
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}Q
.

•

If
Ax^{\infty}_{r}\vdash P=Q
then
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}Q
.

Proof.

Since
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}
and
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
are congruences, it suffices to prove that each axiom is sound, meaning that replacing, in each axiom,
=
by the desired bisimilarity and each variable by a process produces a true statement. Most of these axioms were proven to be sound for the classical notion
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,\!}
of strong bisimilarity
[
17
]
in
[
11
]
. Thus, since both
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}
and
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
are included in
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,\!}
, most of them are sound for
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}
and
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
.

Only the branching axioms, RSP and the reactive approximation axiom remain to be proven sound. The soundness of the branching axioms is trivial and the soundness of RSP is exactly Proposition
21
. For the reactive approximation axiom, it suffices to show that
{\B}:=\;\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}\cup\{(P,Q),(Q,P)\mid\forall X\subseteq A,\psi_{X}(P)\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}\psi_{X}(Q)\}
is a rooted concrete branching time-out bisimulation, as done in Appendix
J
.
∎

5.3
Completeness

A well-known feature of most process algebras is that the standard collection of axioms allows one to bring any guarded process expression in the following normal form
[
1
,
4
]
.

Definition 23
.

Let
P
be a guarded
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
process. The
head-normal form
of
P
is
\hat{P}:=\sum_{\{(\alpha,Q)\mid P\xrightarrow{\alpha}Q\}}\alpha.Q
.

In
[
11
]
, it is proven that the axiomatisation of
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,\!}_{r}
enables one to equate any guarded process with its head-normal form (using a definition of guardedness that is more liberal than the one employed here, with
\tau
allowed as a guard). Since the axiomatisation of
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,\!}_{r}
is included in
Ax^{\infty}
and
Ax^{\infty}_{r}
, this yields the property for them as well.

Lemma 24
.

Let
P
be a guarded
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
process. Then
Ax^{\infty}\vdash P=\hat{P}
and
Ax^{\infty}_{r}\vdash P=\hat{P}
. Moreover,
Ax
or
Ax_{r}
are sufficient if
P
is recursion-free.
∎

This lemma is used extensively in the proof of the following completeness results.

Proposition 25
.

Let
P,Q
be two recursion-free
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
processes. If
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q
(resp.
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{s}_{b}\!}Q
) then, for all
\alpha\in Act
,
Ax_{r}\vdash\alpha.\hat{P}=\alpha.\hat{Q}
(resp.
Ax\vdash\alpha.\hat{P}=\alpha.\hat{Q}
).

Proof.

The
depth

d(p)
of a process
P
is the length of the longest path starting from
P
. Note that it is properly defined for recursion-free processes only. The proof proceeds by induction on
\max(d(P),d(Q))
. The technique is fairly standard and the details can be found in Appendix
K
.
∎

Theorem 26
.

Let
P,Q
be two recursion-free
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
processes. If
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}Q
(resp.
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}Q
) then
Ax_{r}\vdash P=Q
(resp.
Ax\vdash P=Q
).

Proof.

It suffices to express both processes in their head-normal form and then to equate each pair of matching branches using Proposition
25
. Details are in Appendix
K
.
∎

The following theorem lifts this result for
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}
from finite (recursion-free) processes to arbitrary (infinite) ones, subject to the restriction of strong guardedness.

Theorem 27
.

Let
P,Q
be strongly guarded
\mbox{CCSP}^{\theta}_{t}
processes.

If
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{sr}_{b}\!}Q
then
Ax^{\infty}\vdash{P}={Q}
.

Proof.

A well-known technique called
equation merging
can be applied. Details can be found in Appendix
L
.
∎

5.4
Canonical Representative

Unfortunately, equation merging does not work on reactive bisimulations
[
11
]
. Thus, another technique is used
[
14
,
16
]
, called
canonical representatives
. The idea is to build the simplest process for each equivalence class of
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}
and use them as intermediary to equate processes.

Let us denote with
\mathbb{P}^{g}
the strongly guarded fragment of
\mathbb{P}
. For all
P\in\mathbb{P}^{g}
,
[P]:=\{Q\in\mathbb{P}^{g}\mid P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q\}
is the
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
-equivalence class of
P
.
[\mathbb{P}^{g}]
denotes the set of all
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
-equivalence classes. Using the axiom of choice, a choice function
\chi:[\mathbb{P}^{g}]\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{g}
can be defined such that
\forall R\in[\mathbb{P}^{g}],\chi(R)\in R
. A transition relation can be defined between
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
-equivalence classes:

\displaystyle\forall\alpha\in A_{\tau},(R\xrightarrow{\alpha}R^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\;

\displaystyle\chi(R)\Longrightarrow P_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha}P_{2}\wedge P_{1}\in R\wedge P_{2}\in R^{\prime}\wedge(\alpha\in A\vee R\neq R^{\prime}))

\displaystyle R\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}R^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\;

\displaystyle\chi(R)\Longrightarrow P_{1}\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}P_{2}\wedge P_{1}\in R\wedge{P_{1}\!\,\not\!\xrightarrow{\tau}}\wedge P_{2}\in R^{\prime}

All bisimulations can be extended to
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
-equivalence classes. It suffices to consider the set of states
\mathbb{P}^{g}\uplus[\mathbb{P}^{g}]\uplus\{\theta_{X}([P])\mid X\subseteq A\wedge P\in\mathbb{P}^{g}\}
.

Proposition 28
.

Let
P\in\mathbb{P}^{g}
,
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}[P]
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
\B:=\{(P,[P]),([P],P)\mid P\in\mathbb{P}^{g}\}
is a concrete branching time-out bisimulation up to
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
. Details can be found in Appendix
M
.
∎

Definition 29
.

Let
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}^{g}
, the
canonical representative
of
P
and
Q
is a recursive specification
\mathcal{S}
such that
V_{\mathcal{S}}:=\{x_{P},x_{Q}\}\cup\{x_{R}\mid R\in\bigcup_{P^{\prime}\in\mbox{Reach}(P)\cup\mbox{Reach}(Q)}\mbox{Reach}([P^{\prime}])\}
, and
\forall R\in\bigcup_{P^{\prime}\in\mbox{Reach}(P)\cup\mbox{Reach}(Q)}\mbox{Reach}([P^{\prime}])
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{x_{P}}:=\hskip-10.0pt\sum_{\{(\alpha,P^{\prime})\mid P\xrightarrow{\alpha}P^{\prime}\}}\hskip-10.0pt\alpha.x_{[P^{\prime}]}\mbox{ ; }\mathcal{S}_{x_{Q}}:=\hskip-10.0pt\sum_{\{(\alpha,Q^{\prime})\mid Q\xrightarrow{\alpha}Q^{\prime}\}}\hskip-10.0pt\alpha.x_{[Q^{\prime}]}\mbox{ and }\mathcal{S}_{x_{R}}:=\hskip-10.0pt\sum_{\{(\alpha,R^{\prime})\mid R\xrightarrow{\alpha}R^{\prime}\}}\hskip-10.0pt\alpha.x_{R^{\prime}}

The canonical representative is well-defined since
P
,
Q
, as well as processes
[P^{\prime}]\in[\mathbb{P}^{g}]
are finitely branching
[
11
]
. Additionally,
\bigcup_{P^{\prime}\in\mbox{Reach}(P)\cup\mbox{Reach}(Q)}\mbox{Reach}([P^{\prime}])
is countable. Moreover,
\mathcal{S}
is strongly guarded. Furthermore, by construction
\langle x_{R}|\mathcal{S}\rangle\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,\!}R
for all
R\in\bigcup_{P^{\prime}\in\mbox{Reach}(P)\cup\mbox{Reach}(Q)}\mbox{Reach}([P^{\prime}])
.

Proposition 30
.

Let
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}^{g}
and
\mathcal{S}
be the canonical representative of
P
and
Q
.
Ax^{\infty}_{r}\vdash P=\langle x_{P}|\mathcal{S}\rangle
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
P
and
\langle x_{P}|\mathcal{S}\rangle
are
y_{P}
-components of solutions of

\{y_{P^{\dagger}}=\sum_{\{(\alpha,P^{\ddagger})\mid P^{\dagger}\xrightarrow{\alpha}P^{\ddagger}\}}\alpha.y_{P^{\ddagger}}\mid P^{\dagger}\in\mbox{Reach}(P)\}
.
Details can be found in Appendix
N
.
∎

Theorem 31
.

Let
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}^{g}
. If
P\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[7.15547pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cr}_{br}\!}Q
then
Ax^{\infty}_{r}\vdash P=Q
.

Proof.

It suffices to equate
\langle x_{P}|\mathcal{S}\rangle
and
\langle x_{Q}|\mathcal{S}\rangle
using RDP and the reactive approximation axiom. Details can be found in Appendix
N
.
∎

Conclusion

This paper defined a form of branching bisimilarity for processes with time-out transitions, and provided a modal characterisation, congruence results, and a complete axiomatisation for strongly guarded processes. Whereas the bisimilarity presented here treats the time-out action
{\rm t}
more or less as a visible action, in
[
20
]
we propose a variant that elides time-outs, by satisfying laws like
a.{\rm t}.b.0=a.{\rm t}.{\rm t}.b.0
, just like branching bisimilarity elides
\tau
-transitions. We obtained the present paper from
[
20
]
by systemically suppressing this eliding feature, thereby obtaining simpler definitions and proofs.

A topic for future work is to combine this work with the ideas behind
justness

[
12
]
, a weaker form of fairness that allows the formulation and derivation of useful liveness properties. In a setting with time-outs, justness would demand that once a parallel component reaches a state in which a time-out transition is enabled, it cannot stay in that state forever after.

Examples

1

Figure 2:
Counter-Example to a Naive Clause 1.a.

In Figure
2
, the process
a.0+\tau.({\rm t}.b.0+a.b.0)+\tau.(\tau.a.b.0+a.0)
is represented as an LTS. Let
A:=\{a,b\}
. Removing the dashed
a
-transition generates the process
\tau.({\rm t}.b.0+a.b.0)+\tau.(\tau.a.b.0+a.0)
.

First, we are going to show that these two processes are not concrete branching reactive bisimilar. Let’s try to build a concrete branching reactive bisimulation between them. The only way to match the dashed
a
-transition of
a.0+\tau.({\rm t}.0+a.b.0)+\tau.(\tau.a.b.0+a.0)
is by the
a
-transition between
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, because all other
a
-transitions are followed by a
b
-transition. This requires to elide the
\tau
-transition between
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
, who must be concrete branching reactive bisimilar. Since
P_{0}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}P_{1}
, when considering the
\tau
-transition between
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}
,
Q_{0}
has to be concrete branching reactive bisimilar to
P_{1}
or
Q_{1}
. Now, the
a
-transition between
Q_{0}
and
R_{0}
has to be matched by the
a
-transition between
Q_{1}
and
R_{1}
because of the following
b
-transition. This implies
Q_{0}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}Q_{1}
, thus,
Q_{0}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c\emptyset}_{br}\!}Q_{1}
. One has
\mathcal{I}(Q_{0})\cap(\emptyset\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
and
Q_{0}\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}T_{0}
, i.e., when the environment temporary allows no visible actions,
Q_{0}
can time-out into a state in which
b
is possible. This behaviour cannot be matched by
Q_{1}
—a contradiction.

Now, consider the alternative to Definition
1
in which the first clause has been changed to

1.

(a)

if
P\xrightarrow{\tau}P^{\prime}
then there is a path
Q\Longrightarrow Q_{1}\xrightarrow{\mbox{\tiny\rm(}\tau\mbox{\tiny\rm)}}Q_{2}
with
\R(P,Q_{1})
and
\R(P^{\prime},Q_{2})
.

In other words, the scope of the first clause is restricted to
\tau
-transitions. This modification enables building a bisimulation between the two processes. Indeed, the dashed
a
-transition is only considered when the environment allows
a
. Thus, it is sufficient to get
P_{0}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cA}_{br}\!}P_{1}
and
P_{0}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c\{a\}}_{br}\!}P_{1}
and not
P_{0}\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}P_{1}
anymore. Therefore, it is sufficient to match
Q_{0}
and
Q_{1}
in environments allowing
a
. As a result, the outgoing time-out transition of
Q_{0}
is never considered when matching
Q_{0}
with
Q_{1}
, solving our previous issue. Once this observation is made, building the bisimulation is trivial.

Finally, place both processes in the context
\_\!\_\parallel_{\{a\}}(\tau.0+a.0)
. It behaves like a one-way switch enabling to block all
a
-transitions forever as soon as the
\tau
-transition is performed. Let’s try to build a concrete branching reactive bisimulation between the two processes. Following the same reasoning as before, it is necessary to get
P_{0}\parallel_{\{a\}}(\tau.0+a.0)\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cA}_{br}\!}P_{1}\parallel_{\{a\}}(\tau.0+a.0)
because of the dashed
a
-transition, and then
Q_{0}\parallel_{\{a\}}(\tau.0+a.0)\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{cA}_{br}\!}Q_{1}\parallel_{\{a\}}(\tau.0+a.0)
because of the
a
-transition between
Q_{0}
and
R_{0}
. Note that
Q_{0}\parallel_{\{a\}}(\tau.0+a.0)\xrightarrow{\tau}Q_{0}\parallel_{\{a\}}0\linebreak\xrightarrow{{\rm t}}T_{0}\parallel_{\{a\}}0\xrightarrow{b}U_{0}\parallel_{\{a\}}0
and
\mathcal{I}(Q_{0}\parallel_{\{a\}}0)\cap(A\cup\{\tau\})=\emptyset
. As before,
Q_{0}\parallel_{\{a\}}(\tau.0+a.0)
can time-out into a state in which
b
is executable, whereas this behaviour is impossible in
Q_{1}\parallel_{\{a\}}(\tau.0+a.0)
. As a result, restricting the scope of the first clause of Definition
1
to
\tau
-transitions prevents
\mathrel{\,\raisebox{2.15277pt}{$\underline{\makebox[6.99997pt]{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\leftrightarrow$}}}$}\,^{c}_{br}\!}
from being a congruence for parallel composition.

Necessity of the Stability Respecting Clause

P_{0}
P_{2}
P_{1}
P_{3}
